Search for t ​ t ¯ t\bar{t} resonances in final states with exactly one or two leptons using 140 fb -1 of p ​ p pp collision data at s = 13 ​ TeV \sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV} with the ATLAS experiment


Abstract
A search for heavy spin-1 and spin-2 resonances decaying into a top-antitop-quark pair has been performed with 140 fb -1 of proton–proton collision data collected by the ATLAS experiment at the Large Hadron Collider at a centre-of-mass energy of s = 13 ​ TeV \sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV} . Final states with either exactly one electron or muon, or exactly two leptons ( e ​ e ee , μ ​ μ \mu\mu or e ​ μ e\mu ), large missing transverse momentum, and two jets, at least one of which must be identified as likely containing a b -hadron decay, are considered. The search targets resonances with both narrow and broad widths relative to the detector resolution, and with masses in the range of 0.4–5.0 TeV. No significant deviation from the Standard Model prediction is observed. Exclusion limits are set on the production cross-section times branching ratio for hypothetical Z ′ Z^{\prime} bosons, Kaluza–Klein gravitons, and Kaluza–Klein gluons that decay into top-quark pairs.



\AtlasRefCode

EXOT-2018-49

\PreprintIdNumber
CERN-EP-2025-281

\AtlasJournal
JHEP

1
Introduction

New heavy particles coupling strongly to the top quark are predicted in numerous extensions of the Standard Model (SM) of particle physics. Such models are proposed to address open questions of the SM, such as the large difference between the scales of electroweak interactions at
\mathcal{O}(100)

GeV
and gravity at
\mathcal{O}(10^{19})

GeV
, known as the Planck scale
[
1
,
2
]
. Models include, for example, topcolor-assisted-technicolor (TC2)
[
3
,
4
,
5
]
and Randall–Sundrum (RS) models of warped extra dimensions
[
6
,
7
]
, predicting one or several additional heavy bosons, such as a spin-1
Z^{\prime}
particle, a spin-2 Kaluza–Klein (KK) graviton (
G_{\textrm{KK}}
), or a spin-1 KK gluon (
g_{\textrm{KK}}
). The new states can have strong or preferential couplings to the top quark, the heaviest elementary particle of the SM. Resonant production of a top–antitop quark pair (
t\bar{t}
), if observed, would thus provide a smoking-gun signature of phenomena beyond the SM. Searches for this process are therefore an integral part of the experimental programme at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC)
[
8
]
, which now provides unique access to heavy resonances with masses in the multi-
TeV
range.

Searches for heavy resonances decaying into a
t\bar{t}
pair have previously been conducted with the ATLAS and CMS experiments using proton–proton (
pp
) collisions at
\sqrt{s}=7\penalty\ \text{TeV}

[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
, 8
TeV

[
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
, and 13
TeV

[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
. Both experiments have performed the searches in the fully-hadronic and single-lepton final states, while the CMS Collaboration has also included the dileptonic final states. ATLAS and CMS have also recently performed dedicated searches for (pseudo-)scalar resonances
[
24
,
25
]
, focusing on signals with masses below 2
TeV
and where interference between the signal and the SM
t\bar{t}
background is known to be significant. These signals are hence not considered in this paper. In the search performed by CMS, a significant deviation from the SM expected limits for a
t\bar{t}
mass less than approximately 400
GeV
was observed. This was later shown by CMS
[
26
]
to be consistent with a colour-singlet, quasi-bound state of top quarks, predicted in the SM. The mild tension observed by ATLAS
[
27
]
and CMS
[
28
]
between the data and simulation in the quantum entanglement studies is also partially relieved by the inclusion of the pseudo-scalar state. In this paper, the focus is on searches for resonant signals with masses above 400
GeV
; the inclusion of the quasi-bound state as a background was tested and has no impact on the results of this paper.

This paper presents a search for
t\bar{t}
resonances across the
t\bar{t}
invariant mass spectrum,
m_{t\bar{t}}
, from 0.4
TeV
to 5.0
TeV
. The search is performed using 140
fb
-1
of
pp
collision data recorded in 2015–2018 at
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
with the ATLAS detector at the LHC, and uses the single-lepton and dilepton final states, complementing the fully-hadronic ATLAS search. The results in this paper consider spin-1 and spin-2 signal models and are valid for the case when interference is negligible. It is the first time in Run 2 that the dilepton channel has been included in an ATLAS search for these types of resonances, and both the single-lepton and dilepton channels use the full Run-2 data for the first time. Additionally, the single-lepton channel improves on the previous 36
fb
-1
analysis through improved
t\bar{t}
reconstruction and the addition of validation regions to better evaluate the background modelling.

The search presented in this paper targets events in which one top quark decays semi-leptonically and the other hadronically (1-lepton channel), as well as events in which both top quarks decay leptonically (2-lepton channel). Here, leptonic top-quark decays are defined as the top-quark decays resulting in a prompt electron (
e
) or muon (
\mu
), either directly from the
W
boson decay or via secondary decays of leptonically-decaying
\tau
-leptons. Hadronic top decays in the 1-lepton channel are targeted using separate analysis strategies for resolved and merged topologies that are combined to maximise the sensitivity for the
m_{t\bar{t}}
range from 400
GeV
up to values of several
TeV
. In the range
m_{t\bar{t}}>2

TeV
, the top quarks are highly Lorentz boosted, resulting in the decay products merging into a single jet. Therefore, an advanced multivariate classifier based on a deep neural network is used to identify the hadronic top-quark decays with these highly collimated decay products
[
29
]
. The dominant background in both channels arises from SM
t\bar{t}
production.

This paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
, the benchmark models used for the interpretation of the results of this search are introduced. In Sections
3
and
4
, the ATLAS detector as well as the data and simulated event samples are described. The object reconstruction, event selection, categorisation and reconstruction of observables, and the estimate of background processes are described in Sections
5
to
8
. The description of the statistical analysis is provided in Section
9
and the systematic uncertainties are detailed in Section
10
. Finally, results are presented and discussed in Section
11
, where, in the absence of any significant excess, exclusion limits on the production cross-section times branching ratio for the hypothetical new particles are presented at the 95% confidence level.

2
Theoretical framework

The search presented in this paper is designed to be sensitive to the production of different types of spin-1 and spin-2,
s
-channel resonances decaying to
t\bar{t}
. The selection efficiency and acceptance can differ depending on the properties of the new resonance, such as its spin, width, or production mode. Therefore, the sensitivity to three types of new particle was evaluated to quantify the performance of the search. The benchmark models adopted in this search are colour-singlet and colour-octet bosons with spin-1 or spin-2 and masses ranging from 400
GeV
to 5
TeV
. Two benchmark models with a narrow relative resonance width,
\Gamma/m
, below the experimental resolution of
\Gamma/m\approx\mathcal{O}(10\%)
are used, in addition to a model predicting wide resonances with a relative width of
\Gamma/m=30\%
. Figure
1
shows example Feynman diagrams for the three benchmark models considered, and the following section details the theoretical motivation for such models.

((a))

((b))

((c))

Figure 1:

Representative leading-order Feynman diagrams for the
s
-channel production and subsequent decay to
t\bar{t}
of the (a)
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
, (b)
G_{\textrm{KK}}
, and (c)
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals.

2.1
Spin-1 colour singlet

Spin-1, electrically neutral, colour-singlet vector bosons, referred to as
Z^{\prime}
bosons, are predicted in various extensions of the SM, such as top-colour-assisted technicolour models
[
3
,
4
]
. The benchmark model used in this paper is the same as in previous searches for
t\bar{t}
resonances
[
23
,
19
,
20
,
16
,
18
,
10
,
11
]
and is referred to as Model IV in Ref.
[
5
]
. It predicts a leptophobic
Z^{\prime}
boson, referred to as
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
, which is mainly produced via
q\bar{q}
annihilation and decays into first- and third-generation quarks. The properties of the boson are controlled by three parameters: the top-colour tilting parameter,
\cot\theta_{H}
, which controls the width and the production cross-section, and
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, which are related to the coupling to up-type and down-type quarks, respectively. In the benchmark considered in this and previous papers,
f_{1}
is set to 1 and
f_{2}
to 0 to maximise the branching fraction of the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,\rightarrow t\bar{t}
decay, which reaches 33%. The parameter
\cot\theta_{H}
is tuned for each mass point such that the resonance has a width of 3% of its mass
[
5
]
. Given that this width is smaller than the experimental resolution, the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal considered in this paper can also be used to set limits on the production cross-section times branching ratio for a
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal with other widths that are also below the experimental resolution by rescaling the theory production cross-section. The predicted next-to-leading-order (NLO)
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,\rightarrow t\bar{t}
production cross-section for a
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
mass of 4
TeV
is about 7.6 fb for a width of
\Gamma/m=3\%
and about 2.0 fb for a width of
\Gamma/m=1\%
.

The strongest mass constraints on this model have been derived by the ATLAS Collaboration using the search in fully-hadronic final states on 139
fb
-1
of
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
data
[
23
]
. This search excluded
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
bosons with 1% and 3% relative widths with masses up to 3.9
TeV
and 4.7
TeV
, respectively. The CMS Collaboration excluded
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
bosons with masses up to 3.80
TeV
for a decay width of 1% using the combination of all three decay channels on 35.9
fb
-1
of
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
data
[
22
]
. In the 1-lepton decay channel, the ATLAS Collaboration excluded
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signals of width 1% with masses up to 3.0
TeV
, using 36
fb
-1
of data
[
19
]
and no previous search in the 2-lepton decay channel has been performed by the ATLAS Collaboration. Dijet searches performed by ATLAS
[
30
,
31
,
32
]
and CMS
[
33
]
also place constraints on leptophobic
Z^{\prime}
bosons. ATLAS has provided direct sensitivity comparisons in the context of a simplified model with a dark matter
Z^{\prime}
mediator that has flavour-universal couplings to all quarks, and this shows similar sensitivities between flavour-inclusive dijet searches and
t\bar{t}
searches at high masses
[
34
]
. Given that the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
model considered in this paper couples preferentially to top quarks, leading to approximately twice the branching ratio as that to bottom quarks,
t\bar{t}
searches will be more sensitive than dijet searches for models with preferential couplings to
t\bar{t}
.

2.2
Spin-2 colour singlet

Spin-2, colour-singlet bosons are predicted in models that postulate extra dimensions of space leading to Kaluza–Klein (KK) excitations of the graviton. This search considers a Randall-Sundrum (RS) model with a single warped extra dimension, where the SM fields are in the bulk and the fermions are localised appropriately to explain the flavour structure of the SM
[
6
,
35
,
36
]
. The type of KK graviton predicted in this RS model is referred to as “bulk” RS graviton, or
G_{\textrm{KK}}
, and it is characterised by its mass,
m_{G_{KK}}
, and the dimensionless coupling of the KK gravitons to the SM fields,
k/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{Pl}}
, where
\overline{M}_{\mathrm{Pl}}=M_{\mathrm{Pl}}/8\pi
is the reduced Planck mass and
k
is the curvature of the warped extra dimension. Larger values of
k/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{Pl}}
yield a larger production cross-section and a larger resonance width. In RS models with SM fields in the bulk, values of
k/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{Pl}}\approx 1
are favoured
[
35
]
. For these KK gravitons, decays into light fermions are suppressed and the branching ratio to photons is negligible. In this and previous ATLAS papers,
k/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{Pl}}=1.0
, a choice that maximises the production cross-section while keeping the resonance width below the experimental resolution. In the mass range 0.4–3
TeV
considered for the KK graviton in this paper, the width varies between 3% and 6% of the mass. The primary production mode is
gg
fusion, while the primary decay modes are those to
t\bar{t}
,
WW
,
ZZ
, and
hh
. The branching ratio of the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
decay into a
t\bar{t}
pair increases rapidly from 18% to 50% for masses between 0.4 and 0.6
TeV
, reaching a plateau at 68% for masses larger than 1
TeV
.

A previous search in the 1-lepton channel using 36
fb
-1
of ATLAS data
[
19
]
excluded bulk RS gravitons with masses in the range of 0.45–0.65
TeV
. A complementary search on the same ATLAS data sample targeting the decays
G_{KK}\rightarrow WW,ZZ

[
37
]
excluded masses up to 2.3
TeV
. A dijet search
[
30
]
considered the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
benchmark with
k/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{Pl}}=0.2
decaying into a bottom quark–anti-quark pair, and excluded these signals with masses up to 2.8
TeV
. A CMS search on 35.6
fb
-1
of
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
data targeting the decay
G_{KK}\rightarrow ZZ

[
38
]
excluded the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signal with
k/\overline{M}_{\mathrm{Pl}}=0.5
up to 0.8
TeV

1
1

1

The
G_{\textrm{KK}}
benchmark was not considered by the CMS Collaboration in their latest
t\bar{t}
resonance searches
[
22
]
.
.

2.3
Spin-1 colour octet

Spin-1 colour-octet bosons are predicted in models with extra dimensions of space leading to KK excitations of the gluon
[
7
,
39
]
. The benchmark model chosen in this paper, which is consistent with the benchmark choices in previous ATLAS publications, is an RS model with a single warped extra dimension. The primary production mode of these KK gluons, referred to as
g_{\textrm{KK}}
, is via
q\bar{q}
annihilation. The strong coupling of the
g_{\textrm{KK}}
to light quarks is set to
g_{q}=-0.2g_{s}
, where
g_{s}
is the SM gluon coupling to quarks and the couplings correspond to the configuration given in Eq. (3) of Ref.
[
7
]
. The left-handed coupling to the top quark is fixed at
g_{t,\mathrm{L}}=g_{s}
, and the right-handed coupling to the top quark,
g_{t,\mathrm{R}}
, is chosen to obtain a width of 30% to obtain benchmark predictions for a broad resonance. This is fully consistent with the previous ATLAS searches for a
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signal
[
19
,
20
]
. The branching fraction to
t\bar{t}
in this case amounts to 92.5%.

A previous search in the 1-lepton channel using 36
fb
-1
of ATLAS data
[
19
]
excluded KK gluon masses lower than 3.7
TeV
in this benchmark model. The CMS Collaboration searched for similar resonances in 35.9
fb
-1
of
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
data
[
22
]
using a combination of the three
t\bar{t}
decay channels, excluding
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals with masses up to 4.55
TeV
. This is a slightly different benchmark model than that used in this paper, which leads to a natural width of 15–20% and a smaller production cross-section times branching fraction
[
40
]
.

3
ATLAS detector

The ATLAS detector
[
41
]
at the LHC covers nearly the entire solid angle around the collision point.
2
2

2

ATLAS uses a right-handed coordinate system with its origin at the nominal interaction point (IP) in the centre of the detector and the
z
-axis along the beam pipe. The
x
-axis points from the IP to the centre of the LHC ring, and the
y
-axis points upwards. Polar coordinates
(r,\phi)
are used in the transverse plane,
\phi
being the azimuthal angle around the
z
-axis. The pseudorapidity is defined in terms of the polar angle
\theta
as
\eta=-\ln\tan(\theta/2)
and is equal to the rapidity
y=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{E+p_{z}}{E-p_{z}}\right)
in the relativistic limit. Angular distance is measured in units of
\Delta R\equiv\sqrt{(\Delta y)^{2}+(\Delta\phi)^{2}}
.
It consists of an inner tracking detector surrounded by a thin superconducting solenoid, electromagnetic and hadronic calorimeters, and a muon spectrometer incorporating three large superconducting air-core toroidal magnets.

The inner-detector system (ID) is immersed in a
2\text{\,}\mathrm{T}
axial magnetic field and provides charged-particle tracking in the range
|\eta|<2.5
. The high-granularity silicon pixel detector covers the vertex region and typically provides four measurements per track, the first hit generally being in the insertable B-layer (IBL) installed before Run 2
[
42
,
43
]
. It is followed by the SemiConductor Tracker (SCT), which usually provides eight measurements per track. These silicon detectors are complemented by the transition radiation tracker (TRT), which enables radially extended track reconstruction up to
|\eta|=2.0
. The TRT also provides electron identification information
based on the fraction of hits (typically 30 in total) above a higher energy-deposit threshold corresponding to transition radiation.

The calorimeter system covers the pseudorapidity range
|\eta|<4.9
. Within the region
|\eta|<3.2
, electromagnetic calorimetry is provided by barrel and endcap high-granularity lead/liquid-argon (LAr) calorimeters, with an additional thin LAr presampler covering
|\eta|<1.8
to correct for energy loss in material upstream of the calorimeters. Hadronic calorimetry is provided by the steel/scintillator-tile calorimeter, segmented into three barrel structures within
|\eta|<1.7
, and two copper/LAr hadronic endcap calorimeters. The solid angle coverage is completed with forward copper/LAr and tungsten/LAr calorimeter modules optimised for electromagnetic and hadronic energy measurements, respectively.

The muon spectrometer (MS) comprises separate trigger and high-precision tracking chambers measuring the deflection of muons in a magnetic field generated by the superconducting air-core toroidal magnets. The field integral of the toroids ranges between
2.0
and
6.0\text{\,}\mathrm{T}\text{\,}\mathrm{m}
across most of the detector. Three layers of precision chambers, each consisting of layers of monitored drift tubes, cover the region
|\eta|<2.7
, complemented by cathode-strip chambers in the forward region, where the background is highest. The muon trigger system covers the range
|\eta|<2.4
with resistive-plate chambers in the barrel, and thin-gap chambers in the endcap regions.

The luminosity is measured mainly by the LUCID–2
[
44
]
detector that records Cherenkov light produced in the quartz windows of photomultipliers located close to the beampipe.

Events were selected by the first-level trigger system implemented in custom hardware, followed by selections made by algorithms implemented in software in the high-level trigger
[
45
]
. The first-level trigger accepted events from the
40\text{\,}\mathrm{MHz}
bunch crossings at a rate close to
100\text{\,}\mathrm{kHz}
, which the high-level trigger further reduced in order to record complete events to disk at about
1.25\text{\,}\mathrm{kHz}
.

A software suite
[
46
]
is used in data simulation, in the reconstruction and analysis of real and simulated data, in detector operations, and in the trigger and data acquisition systems of the experiment.

4
Data and simulated event samples

Data from
pp
collisions at
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
corresponding to an integrated luminosity of 140
fb
-1
, collected in the years 2015 to 2018 with the ATLAS detector, are analysed in the context of this search. The uncertainty in the combined 2015–2018 integrated luminosity is 0.83%
[
47
]
, obtained primarily using the LUCID-2 detector
[
44
]
, and complemented by measurements provided by the inner detector and the calorimeters. The average number of interactions per bunch crossing (pile-up) in this data sample is
\langle\mu\rangle

=33.7
. All detector subsystems were required to be operational during data taking
[
48
]
.

Candidate events for both the 1- and 2-lepton channels are selected using single-muon
[
49
]
and single-electron
[
50
]
triggers. These triggers require a muon or an electron with momentum transverse to the beamlime,
p_{\textrm{T}}
, or energy transverse to the beamline,
E_{\text{T}}
, respectively, above a certain threshold and satisfying certain quality and, for some triggers, lepton isolation requirements. The triggers with the lowest
p_{\textrm{T}}
(
E_{\text{T}}
) threshold require
p_{\textrm{T}}(E_{\text{T}})>26

GeV
for muons (electrons) in 2016–2018 and
p_{\textrm{T}}(E_{\text{T}})>20(24)

GeV
in 2015, and include a lepton isolation requirement that is not applied for triggers with higher thresholds. The trigger efficiency is mostly constant in the transverse momentum for leptons with
p_{\textrm{T}}>28

GeV
, but can vary with
\eta
.

Monte Carlo (MC) simulated event samples are used to model the signal and background processes. Unless otherwise stated, the top quark mass was always set to
m_{\mathrm{top}}=172.5

GeV
; samples that used
Pythia

[
51
]
for the generation used the A14 set of tuned parameters (tune)
[
52
]
with the
NNPDF2.3lo

[
53
]
set of parton distribution functions (PDFs); and all samples that did not use
Sherpa

[
54
]
used
EvtGen

[
55
]
to model the decays of bottom and charm hadrons. The ATLAS simulation infrastructure
[
46
]
was used to simulate the detector and its response. Signal and nominal background samples as well as several alternative background samples used to assess systematic uncertainties were produced with a detailed
Geant4

[
56
]
detector simulation. A faster simulation based on a parameterisation of the calorimeter response and
Geant4
for the other detector systems
[
46
]
was used for the remaining alternative background samples. Pile-up effects were modelled by overlaying minimum-bias events simulated using the soft QCD processes of
Pythia
8.186 with the
NNPDF2.3lo
set of PDFs and the A3 tune
[
57
]
. The pile-up profiles match the ones of each data sample between 2015 and 2018. The same offline reconstruction methods used for data were applied to the simulated event samples. Corrections were applied to the simulated events to match the selection efficiencies, energy and mass scales and resolutions of reconstructed simulated particles to those measured in data control samples. An overview of the generator choices for the signal and background processes can be found in Table
1
, with further details given in the following.

The dominant background process is SM
t\bar{t}
production. Smaller background components arise from single vector-boson (
W
,
Z
) production in association with hadronic jets, referred to as
W/Z+\mathrm{jets}
, and single top-quark production (single top). There are also background processes in both channels that lead to at least one mis-identified or non-prompt lepton that satisfies the lepton identification and isolation criteria applied in this search, referred to as
fakes
in this paper. These mostly originate from strong multijet production in the 1-lepton channel and from single-lepton
t\bar{t}
events in the 2-lepton channel. Single top and
W+\mathrm{jets}
production can also lead to these mis-identified or non-prompt leptons. Minor backgrounds from
t\bar{t}+V
,
t\bar{t}+H
and diboson (
WW
,
ZZ
,
WZ
) production are also considered. All background components, except the fakes in the 1-lepton channel (see Section
8.2
), are modelled using MC simulation. Data-driven corrections are applied to the MC-based estimates of the
W+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 1-lepton channel (see Section
8.1
) and the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 2-lepton channel (see Section
8.3
) to correct the
W+\mathrm{jets}
normalisation and
Z+\mathrm{jets}
shape, respectively.

SM
t\bar{t}
production was generated at NLO accuracy in QCD using
Powheg Box
v2
[
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
,
62
]
with the
NNPDF3.0nlo

[
63
]
PDF set and the
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter set to 1.5
m_{\mathrm{top}}

[
64
]
.
3
3

3

The
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter controls the
p_{\textrm{T}}
of the first additional emission beyond the leading-order Feynman diagram in the parton shower and therefore regulates the high-
p_{\textrm{T}}
emission against which the
t\bar{t}
system recoils.
The functional form of the renormalisation and factorisation scales was set to the default scale
\sqrt{m_{\textrm{top}}^{2}+p_{\textrm{T}}^{2}}
. The top quarks were decayed in
Powheg Box
, thus preserving their spin correlations. The matrix element (ME) generator is interfaced with
Pythia
8.230
[
65
]
to model the parton shower (PS), hadronisation, and the underlying event (UE).

Alternative SM
t\bar{t}
samples obtained with different generator choices and settings are used to estimate systematic uncertainties related to the modelling of this main background component. The details can be found in Section
10.1
.

The top-quark kinematics in the nominal and all alternative SM
t\bar{t}
samples were corrected to more accurate differential predictions calculated at next-to-next-to-leading-order (NNLO) QCD and NLO electroweak (EW) accuracy for a top-quark mass value of
m_{\mathrm{top}}
=173.3
GeV

[
66
]
. The difference between the
m_{\mathrm{top}}
value used in the reweighting and the value of 172.5
GeV
used in the NLO MC generation was found to have no impact on the analysis sensitivity. The corrections were applied as event-by-event weights that were obtained via an iterative recursive reweighting procedure in the differential distributions of the (anti-)top
p_{\textrm{T}}
and
m_{t\bar{t}}
, referred to as
NNLO reweighting
, following the same procedure as described in Ref.
[
24
]
. The systematic uncertainties related to the NNLO reweighting procedure are listed in Section
10.1
.

All reweighted
t\bar{t}
samples were additionally normalised to the cross-section prediction at NNLO in QCD, including the resummation of next-to-next-to-leading logarithmic (NNLL) soft-gluon terms calculated using
Top++
2.0
[
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
]
, corresponding to
\mathrm{832\pm 51\penalty\ pb}
.

Single top production in the
Wt
-channel
[
74
]
, which constitutes the main single top contribution, was generated with
Powheg Box
v2 using the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set. The overlap between
t\bar{t}
and
Wt
production was treated within the diagram removal (DR) scheme
[
75
]
and the renormalisation and factorisation scales were set to
H_{\mathrm{T}}/2
, where
H_{\mathrm{T}}
denotes the scalar sum of the transverse momenta of all final-state particles in the event. The ME generator was interfaced with
Pythia
8.307
[
76
]
for the PS, hadronisation, and the UE modelling. The sample was normalised to the theory cross-sections for inclusive
Wt
, calculated at NLO accuracy in QCD with NNLL soft-gluon corrections and corresponding to
71.7\pm 3.8\,$\mathrm{pb}$

[
77
]
.

Single top production in the
s
-channel
[
78
]
was generated with
Powheg Box
v2 at NLO in QCD using the five-flavour scheme and the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set. Single top production in the
t
-channel was generated with
Powheg Box
v2
[
79
]
, using the four-flavour scheme for the NLO ME calculations together with the corresponding PDF
NNPDF3.0
set. For this process, the top-quark decays were simulated using
MadSpin

[
80
]
, preserving all spin correlations. For both processes, the ME generator was also interfaced with
Pythia
8.230. The inclusive cross-sections were corrected to the theory predictions at NLO in QCD with

Hathor
2.1
[
81
,
82
]
. For the
s
-channel process, this cross-section corresponds to
6.35^{+0.23}_{-0.20}\,$\mathrm{pb}$
(
3.97^{+0.19}_{-0.17}\,$\mathrm{pb}$
) for single top (single anti-top) production
[
83
]
, and for the
t
-channel process, this cross-section corresponds to
136.0^{+5.4}_{-4.6}
pb (
81.0^{+4.1}_{-3.6}
pb)
[
84
]
for single top (single anti-top) production.

The background from
t\bar{t}+V
(
V=W,Z
) production was generated at NLO accuracy in QCD with
MadGraph5_aMC@NLO
2.3.3
[
85
]
with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set, interfaced with
Pythia
8.210 for the PS, hadronisation, and UE. The samples were normalised to the theory cross-section calculated at NLO in QCD
[
86
]
. The background from
t\bar{t}+H
production was generated at NLO accuracy in QCD with
Powheg Box
v2 with the
NNPDF3.0nlo
PDF set, interfaced with
Pythia
8.230 for the PS, hadronisation, and UE. The samples were normalised to the theory cross-section calculated at NLO QCD and NLO EW accuracies
[
86
]
.

The production of a single
W
boson in association with hadronic jets (
W+\mathrm{jets}
) was simulated with
Sherpa
2.2.11. The background from
Z+\mathrm{jets}
production was simulated with
Sherpa
2.2.11 in the 1-lepton channel and with
Sherpa
2.2.1 in the 2-lepton channel. NLO ME calculations were used for up to two partons for all samples, and leading-order (LO) ME calculations for up to five (four) partons for the
Sherpa
2.2.11 (
Sherpa
2.2.1) samples. The MEs were calculated with the
Comix

[
87
]
and
OpenLoops

[
88
,
89
,
90
]
libraries. They were matched with the
Sherpa
PS
[
91
]
using the
MEPS@NLO
prescription
[
92
,
93
,
94
,
95
]
and the set of tuned parameters developed by the
Sherpa
authors. The
NNPDF3.0nnlo
set of PDFs was used for all
V+\mathrm{jets}
samples. The
W+\mathrm{jets}
and
Z+\mathrm{jets}
samples were normalised to the theory cross-sections calculated at NLO accuracy in QCD
[
96
]
. An additional data-driven normalisation correction was derived for the
W+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 1-lepton channel. Details can be found in Section
8.1
.

Diboson events with fully leptonic and semileptonic decays were simulated using
Sherpa
2.2.1 and
Sherpa
2.2.2
[
97
]
, respectively. The simulation includes off-shell effects and Higgs boson contributions, where appropriate. Events were simulated using ME calculations at NLO accuracy in QCD for up to one additional parton and at LO accuracy for up to three additional parton emissions. Samples for the loop-induced processes
gg\rightarrow VV
were simulated using LO-accurate ME calculations for up to one additional parton emission. The ME calculations were matched and merged with the
Sherpa
PS based on the Catani–Seymour dipole factorisation
[
87
,
91
]
using the MEPS@NLO prescription. The virtual QCD corrections were provided by the
OpenLoops
library. The
NNPDF3.0nnlo
set of PDFs was used, along with the dedicated set of tuned PS parameters developed by the
Sherpa
authors. The cross-sections from the generator were used for sample normalisation.

The fakes background in the 2-lepton channel is estimated using the single-lepton
t\bar{t}
MC sample, the single top MC samples and the
W+\mathrm{jets}
MC sample that contains two leptons. After requiring two reconstructed leptons, if at least one of the leptons is not matched to a prompt lepton at generator level, the event is considered to be part of the fakes background.

The production of each of the three signals decaying into
t\bar{t}
was modelled at LO in QCD with the
NNPDF2.3lo
PDF set using
MadGraph
2.9.3 for the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
and
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals, and
MadGraph
2.2.3 for the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signal.
Pythia
8.245 was used to model the PS and hadronisation of the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
and
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals, and
Pythia
8.210 was used for the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signal. For the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signals, the generated values of the cross-section were scaled to the cross-section predictions obtained at NLO in QCD for the 1.2% width
[
98
]
, whilst the cross-sections obtained with
MadGraph
were used directly for the
g_{\textrm{KK}}
and
G_{\textrm{KK}}
samples.

5
Object reconstruction

Common event-quality criteria and object definitions are applied for both analysis channels, including standard data-quality requirements to select data events with the detector in good operating condition
[
48
]
. In addition, in each analysis channel, dedicated event selection criteria, which are specific to the objects and kinematics of interest in those final states, are applied as described in Section
6
.

Events are required to have at least one reconstructed
pp
interaction vertex with a minimum of two associated tracks with
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0.5

GeV
. The primary vertex matched to the hard-scatter of the interaction is defined as the vertex with the highest sum of squared transverse momenta of associated tracks
[
99
]
. A set of baseline quality criteria are applied to reject events with non-collision backgrounds or detector noise
[
100
]
.

Two levels of object identification requirements are defined for charged leptons and jets: baseline and signal. Baseline leptons and jets are selected with looser identification criteria, and are used in computing the missing transverse momentum and in resolving possible reconstruction ambiguities. Signal leptons and jets are a subset of the baseline objects with tighter quality requirements, which are used to define the search regions. Isolation criteria are used to discriminate between prompt leptons and leptons arising from semileptonic heavy-flavour decays or jets mis-identified as leptons.

Jets are reconstructed from particle-flow objects
[
101
]
using the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm
[
102
,
103
]
with a radius parameter
R=0.4
. The particle-flow algorithm combines information about ID tracks and energy deposits in the calorimeters to form the input for jet reconstruction. A pile-up subtraction procedure
[
104
]
is applied along with a global sequential calibration to account for flavour dependencies. To suppress jets arising from pile-up, a jet-vertex-tagging (JVT) technique using a multivariate likelihood
[
105
]
is applied to jets with
p_{\mathrm{T}}<60

GeV
and
|\eta|<2.4
to reject jets that do not orginate from the hard-scatter primary vertex. Baseline jets are selected by requiring
p_{\mathrm{T}}>20

GeV
and
|\eta|<4.5
. Signal jets are selected via the tighter requirements
p_{\mathrm{T}}>30

GeV
and
|\eta|<2.5
.

Jets with a larger radius parameter,
R=1.0
, referred to as large-
R
jets in the following, are used as proxies for hadronically-decaying top quarks with a large Lorentz boost. They are reconstructed from three-dimensional topological clusters of energy deposits in the calorimeter using the anti-
k_{t}
algorithm. The topological clusters are calibrated to the hadronic energy scale with the local cluster weighting (LCW)
[
106
]
procedure to account for the non-compensating calorimeter response and the energy loss in dead material or due to out-of-cluster leakage of deposited energy. The large-
R
jets are trimmed
[
107
]
to reduce contributions from pile-up and soft interactions by reclustering the jet constituents into subjets, using the
k_{t}
algorithm
[
108
,
109
,
110
]
with a radius parameter
R=0.2
, and discarding constituents belonging to subjets with
p_{\textrm{T}}
less than 5% of the
p_{\textrm{T}}
of the parent jet
[
111
]
. The four-momenta of large-
R
jets are reconstructed from the momentum vectors of the remaining subjets and calibrated using simulation
[
112
]
. The large-
R
jets used are required to have
p_{\mathrm{T}}>350

GeV
and
|\eta|<2.0
.

Large-
R
jets originating from a boosted hadronically-decaying top quarks are identified using a multivariate classification algorithm based on a deep neural network (DNN)
[
29
]
that takes 13 jet moments, including the jet mass and
p_{\textrm{T}}
, as inputs for training. A DNN score between zero and one is obtained, with top-quark-initiated jets having values close to one and light-quark- and gluon-initiated jets having values close to zero. The top-tagging requirements used are optimised for “inclusive” jets containing some but not necessarily all of the decay products of the hadronically-decaying top quark. A large-
R
jet is considered “top-tagged” using a requirement on the DNN output score that corresponds to an identification efficiency of 80% for inclusive top jets, evaluated in simulated SM
t\bar{t}
events. The rejection factor for light-flavour quark and gluon jets varies as a function of the large-
R
jet
p_{\textrm{T}}
, from approximately 60 at 350
GeV
to approximately 10 at 3000
GeV
, where the rejection factor is defined as the reciprocal of the probability of mistakenly identifying a light-quark-initiated jet or a gluon-initiated jet as a top-quark-initiated jet. Scale factors are applied to all jets satisfying the top-tagging requirement in simulated events to correct for possible efficiency differences between simulated event samples and data
[
29
]
.

Small-
R
jets in the range
|\eta|<2.5
are identified as likely to contain a
b
-hadron, henceforth called
b
-tagged, using the DL1r algorithm
[
113
]
. This algorithm is based on a multivariate classification technique with a DNN combining information from the impact parameters of tracks and topological properties of secondary and tertiary decay vertices reconstructed from the tracks associated with the jet. The
b
-tagged jets are selected using an operating point corresponding to an efficiency of 77% for identifying true
b
-jets in simulated SM
t\bar{t}
events. This operating point corresponds to a rejection factor of 6 for charm and of 134 for light-flavour jets. Correction factors are applied to the simulated event samples to compensate for differences between data and simulation in the
b
-tagging efficiency for
b
-,
c
-, and light-flavour jets
[
114
,
115
,
116
]
. The correction for
b
-jets is derived from
t\bar{t}
events with final states containing two leptons, and the corrections are consistent with unity within uncertainties at the level of a few percent over most of the jet
p_{\textrm{T}}
range. The corrections for
c
-jets and light-flavour jets are derived in semi-leptonic
t\bar{t}
events and
Z+\mathrm{jets}
events, respectively.

Muon candidates are reconstructed from matching tracks in the ID and MS, refined through a global fit that uses the hits from both subdetectors
[
117
]
. Baseline muons must have
p_{\mathrm{T}}>10

GeV
and
|\eta|<2.5
, and satisfy a set of medium identification criteria. Additionally, the longitudinal impact parameter is required to satisfy
|z_{0}\sin\theta|<0.5
mm. Signal muons are required to have
p_{\mathrm{T}}>25

GeV
and additionally satisfy the following requirement on the transverse impact parameter
d_{0}
and its uncertainty
\sigma_{d_{0}}
:
|d_{0}/\sigma_{d_{0}}|<3
. They are also required to be isolated using the requirement that the sum of the transverse momenta of the tracks within a variable-radius cone around the muon direction, excluding the muon track, be less than 6% of the transverse momentum of the muon
[
117
]
. The track isolation cone size is given by the minimum of
R=10\;\mathrm{GeV}/p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mu}
and
R=0.3
, where
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mu}
is the muon
p_{\text{T}}

[
16
]
.

Electron candidates are reconstructed from energy deposits in the electromagnetic calorimeter matched to a charged-particle track in the ID
[
118
]
. The track is required to be matched to the primary vertex by imposing the requirement
|z_{0}\sin\theta|<0.5
mm. Electron candidates are required to be within
|\eta|<2.47
, excluding the transition region between the barrel and endcap calorimeters (
1.37<|\eta|<1.52
). Baseline electrons are required to satisfy
p_{\mathrm{T}}>10

GeV
and fulfil loose identification criteria, using a likelihood-based discriminant that combines information about tracks in the ID and energy deposits in the calorimeter system
[
118
]
. The number of hits in the innermost pixel layer is used to discriminate between electrons and converted photons. Signal electrons are required to also satisfy
p_{\mathrm{T}}>25

GeV
and the tight likelihood identification criteria
[
118
]
. Additionally, signal electrons must fulfil
|d_{0}/\sigma(d_{0})|<5
. The same variable-cone isolation requirement as with muons is imposed on signal electrons, with the exception that the maximum cone radius is set to 0.2.

An overlap removal procedure is applied to resolve the reconstruction ambiguities between electrons, muons, and small-
R
jets. First, if an electron shares the same ID track with another electron, the electron with the lower transverse momentum is discarded. Electron candidates sharing the same inner-detector track as a muon are rejected, as they are likely misidentified energy deposits from bremsstrahlung photons emitted by the muon. Next, the closest jet to an electron is rejected if it lies within
\Delta R=0.2
of the electron. Similarly, jets are rejected if they are within
\Delta R=0.2
of a muon if the jet has fewer than three associated tracks or the muon is matched to the jet through ghost association
[
119
]
. Next, to reduce the background contributions due to muons from heavy-flavour decays inside jets, muons are discarded if they are separated from the nearest jet by
\Delta R<0.04+10\;\mathrm{GeV}/p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mu}
. For electrons, an electron-in-jet subtraction method based on that employed in Ref.
[
16
]
is used to suppress backgrounds from leptonic heavy-flavour decays inside jets, while maintaining a high reconstruction efficiency for electrons from highly collimated leptonic decays of boosted top quarks, which typically result in an electron close to a
b
-jet. If an electron is found within
\Delta R=0.4
of a jet, the four-momentum of the electron is subtracted from that of the jet, and the
p_{\textrm{T}}
of the electron-subtracted jet is calculated. A
p_{\textrm{T}}
threshold is defined using simulated
Z\rightarrow ee
events and if the electron-subtracted jet has a
p_{\textrm{T}}
below this threshold, the jet is discarded and the electron is retained. If the electron-subtracted jet has a
p_{\textrm{T}}
above this threshold, the electron is discarded and the original jet is retained if the
\Delta R
between the electron and the electron-subtracted jet is less than 0.2. Both the electron and the electron-subtracted jet are retained if the
\Delta R
between them is greater than 0.2.

The missing transverse momentum
\vec{p}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
, with magnitude
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
, is calculated as the negative vectorial sum of the transverse momenta of all baseline reconstructed objects (electrons, muons, small-
R
jets and photons
[
118
]
) and a soft term. The soft term includes all tracks associated with the primary vertex but not matched to any reconstructed physics object. Tracks not associated with the primary vertex are not considered in the
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
calculation, improving the
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
resolution by suppressing the effect of pile-up
[
120
]
.

6
Event selection and categorisation

Events satisfying the single-electron and single-muon trigger requirements outlined in Section
4
, and the data-quality requirements described in Section
5
, are classified as belonging to the 1- or 2-lepton channels based on the number of signal electrons or muons they contain. The lepton requirements applied in this context are designed to ensure orthogonality between the two channels. Events in the 1-lepton channel are further classified into orthogonal event categories based on whether their detector signature is compatible with the presence of either a merged or resolved hadronic top-quark decay, and based on the number of associated
b
-jets. Angular requirements are used to define orthogonal signal and validation regions in both the 1- and 2-lepton channels. The latter have a similar background composition to the corresponding signal regions but are depleted in the expected signal processes. They serve to validate the modelling of the dominant background from SM
t\bar{t}
production.

6.1
Event selection and categorisation in the 1-lepton channel

Events are required to contain exactly one signal electron or muon with a minimum
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\ell}>28

GeV
. This threshold is chosen to ensure that events are selected from the trigger efficiency plateau. The electron or muon is also required to be matched, within
\Delta R<0.15
, to the respective trigger object. Events with a second signal electron or muon (with
p_{\mathrm{T}}>25

GeV
, see Section
5
) are vetoed. Regions are not split according to the flavour of the selected lepton because it was found that such a splitting would not increase the sensitivity of the analysis.

The detector signature of a
t\bar{t}
decay in the 1-lepton channel involves the presence of a substantial amount of missing transverse momentum from the leptonically-decaying
W
boson. To suppress background from the fakes that can cause small amounts of
E_{\text{T}}^{\text{miss}}
via leptonic decays in heavy-flavour jets or mis-measured jet energies, the requirement
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\textrm{miss}}>20

GeV
is imposed. Additionally, events are required to fulfil
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\textrm{miss}}+m_{\mathrm{T}}^{W}>60

GeV
, where the transverse mass of the selected lepton and the
\vec{p}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
, referred to as the
W
transverse mass, or
m_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{W}}
, is defined as:

m_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{W}}=\sqrt{2p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\ell}E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\textrm{miss}}(1-\cos\Delta\phi(p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\ell},\vec{p}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\textrm{miss}}))}\,.

All events must contain at least one
b
-tagged small-
R
jet.

Events are further categorised based on their detector signature as containing either a merged hadronic top-quark decay (“merged category”), reconstructed as a single large-
R
jet, or a resolved hadronic top-quark decay (“resolved category”), resulting in three individual (small-
R
) jets. To obtain orthogonal signal regions, only events that do not meet the requirements for the merged-topology selection are considered for the resolved-topology selection. The preference for the merged- over the resolved-topology selection is based on its superior mtt resolution (see Section
7.1
).

Events in the merged category must contain at least one small-
R
jet with a distance
\Delta R<2.0
to the selected lepton. If only one jet is found, it is identified as the
b
-jet candidate from the leptonic top-quark decay. If more than one jet is found within
\Delta R<2.0
from the selected lepton, the
b
-jet candidate is chosen as the jet with the highest
p_{\textrm{T}}
among all
b
-tagged candidate jets. If none of the candidate jets is
b
-tagged, the jet with the highest
p_{\textrm{T}}
is chosen instead. In addition, exactly one top-tagged large-
R
jet must be present in the event with a distance
\Delta R>1.5
to the lepton and to the selected
b
-jet candidate from the leptonic top-quark decay.

Only events failing the merged selection are considered for the resolved-topology selection. A fully resolved hadronic top-quark decay is expected to result in three small-
R
jets, one of which is a
b
-jet, in addition to a
b
-jet from the leptonic top-quark decay. Hence, events with a resolved decay topology are required to contain at least four selected small-
R
jets. The
t\bar{t}
system is then reconstructed using a
\chi^{2}
algorithm, defined in Section
7.1.1
. Only well-reconstructed events, defined via the requirement
\textrm{log}_{10}(\chi^{2})<0.9
, are kept for further analysis. This requirement has an efficiency of around 60% for SM
t\bar{t}
events. All selection criteria are summarised in Table
2
.

In Figure
2
, the selection efficiency times acceptance for the
\ell+\mathrm{jets}
final state is shown for the merged, resolved and combined categories, separately for the three benchmark scenarios described in Section
2
. It is given as a function of
m_{t\bar{t}}^{\mathrm{beforeFSR}}
, which is defined using the invariant mass of the top and anti-top quarks at parton level, before final-state radiation (FSR), and allows access to the invariant mass of the partonic system immediately from the resonance decay. The product of the acceptance times efficiency is defined as the ratio of events satisfying the signal region requirements to the total number of generated events. For reference, the branching ratio for
t\bar{t}
to
e+\mathrm{jets}
or
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
final states is about 17% for each lepton flavour, taking into account leptonic
\tau
-lepton decays
[
121
]
. The efficiency times acceptance is dominated by the merged topology selection for
t\bar{t}
invariant mass values
m_{t\bar{t}}>1000

GeV
, while the resolved topology selection covers the region down to the kinematic threshold of the analysis at 400
GeV
. The selection efficiency times acceptance has a comparable
m_{t\bar{t}}
dependence for all three benchmark models with small differences arising due to differences between the spin of the intermediate resonance, which leads to differences in the angular distributions of the
t\bar{t}
decay products. The values vary from around 3% for the lowest mass signals up to around 12% for signals with masses between about 1 and 1.5
TeV
. They are consistent within a few % between the different signal hypotheses.

((b))

((c))

\ell+\mathrm{jets}
final state as a function of the
t\bar{t}
invariant mass at the parton level before the emission of FSR, for the

Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
,

G_{\textrm{KK}}
, and

g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals. The selections in the resolved topology only (short-dashed magenta), the merged topology only (dashed blue) and the inclusive topology (solid black) are shown. The error bars correspond to the statistical uncertainty. All
t\bar{t}
decay modes are considered.

To further increase the search sensitivity, events passing the resolved topology selection are divided into separate sub-categories based on whether one or both of the reconstructed top quarks have an associated
b
-tagged jet. The matching of
b
-tagged jets to the leptonic or hadronic top quark is performed by checking whether the small-
R
jet assigned as the
b
-candidate jet to the leptonic side or one of the small-
R
jets used for the reconstruction of the hadronic decay (based on the result of the
\chi^{2}
minimisation) is
b
-tagged. The
Resolved
2b
category contains events in which both top-quark candidates have associated
b
-tagged jets, while the
Resolved
1b
category contains events in which only the leptonically- or hadronically-decaying top-quark candidate has an associated
b
-tagged jet. No
b
-tagging categorisation is used for the merged topology as it provides minimal improvement in sensitivity.

The three 1-lepton categories (Merged, Resolved 2
b
, and Resolved 1
b
) are further sub-divided into orthogonal signal and validation regions based on the pseudorapidity difference between the selected lepton and the hadronic top quark,
\Delta\eta\left(\ell,t^{\textrm{had}}\right)
. The validation regions have a similar background composition as their corresponding signal regions; however, they are depleted in signal events, which typically exhibit smaller values of
\Delta\eta\left(\ell,t^{\textrm{had}}\right)
. These validation regions serve to validate the modelling of the dominant background from SM
t\bar{t}
production before the hypothesis testing in data. The
\Delta\eta\left(\ell,t^{\textrm{had}}\right)
values chosen to define the signal and validation regions depend on the
m_{t\bar{t}}
range and are summarised in Table
3
.

. Values larger than those listed in the table correspond instead to the validation regions.

The highest SM
t\bar{t}
contribution (94.9%) is found in the Resolved
2b
region due to the requirement of two
b
-tagged jets. In the Resolved
1b
and Merged signal regions, the SM
t\bar{t}
production contributes to 79.2% and 88.9%, respectively, of the total SM background.

Additional control and validation regions enriched in hadronic jets falsely identified as leptons are defined for the data-driven estimate of the fakes background via the matrix method
[
122
]
. These are obtained by inverting the
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
and/or
m_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{W}}
requirements of the signal regions and additionally loosening the lepton identification and isolation requirements. Details are given in Section
8.2
.

6.2
Event selection and categorisation in the 2-lepton channel

Candidate events for the 2-lepton channel are required to have exactly two signal leptons and at least two reconstructed jets. At least one of these leptons is required to have
p_{\mathrm{T}}>28

GeV
and to match, within
\Delta R<0.15
, the lepton of the same flavour reconstructed by the trigger algorithm. Additionally, at least one of the jets is required to be
b
-tagged. Depending on the flavour of the charged leptons, three channels are defined:
ee
,
\mu\mu
and
e\mu
. Events satisfying these requirements are then further classified into signal and validation regions.

Events are required to contain two leptons with opposite-sign electric charge (OS). In the
ee
and
\mu\mu
channels, to further suppress the background from
Z/\gamma^{*}+\mathrm{jets}
production, the dilepton invariant mass,
m_{\ell\ell}
, is required to be greater than 15
GeV
and not within the range 81–101
GeV
(excluding the
Z
-boson pole). The missing transverse momentum is also required to be larger than 45
GeV
. Additionally, events must meet the requirement that the invariant masses of the lepton–
b
-jet pairs,
m_{\ell b}
, be smaller than 150
GeV
for at least one of the two possible
b
-jet to lepton assignments. In this context, the two
b
-jets are either taken as the two
b
-tagged jets with the largest
p_{\textrm{T}}
or, if only one jet in the event is
b
-tagged, the
b
-tagged jet and the jet with the highest
p_{\textrm{T}}
among the jets that are not
b
-tagged. This requirement is meant to veto events where at least one of the lepton–
b
-jet pairs does not originate from a top-quark decay. It effectively suppresses events from
Wt
production and
t\bar{t}
events in which at least one of the selected
b
-jets does not originate from a top-quark decay but, for example, from initial-state radiation (ISR). It has a 64% efficiency for SM
t\bar{t}
events with both top quarks decaying leptonically, and a 68% efficiency for signal events with a resonance mass of 1000
GeV
. The event selection requirements are summarised in Table
4
and the relative contribution of SM
t\bar{t}
production to the total SM background amounts to 94.7%.

Five orthogonal regions based on the value of the azimuthal angle between the two leptons,
\Delta\phi_{\ell\ell}
, divided by

are defined: [0, 0.50], (0.50, 0.80], (0.80, 0.90], (0.90, 0.95], and (0.95, 1.00]. The
\frac{\Delta\phi_{\ell\ell}}{\pi}
values used to define these regions have been chosen to optimise the sensitivity of the 2-lepton channel to spin-1 resonances, while ensuring that each region contains enough data.

In analogy to the 1-lepton channel, events in these five regions are further divided into orthogonal signal and validation regions based on the pseudorapidity difference between the two selected leptons,
\Delta\eta_{\ell\ell}
. The
\Delta\eta_{\ell\ell}
values chosen to define the signal and validation regions depend on the
m_{\ell\ell bb}
range and are summarised in Table
5
.

In addition to the signal and validation regions defined above, an orthogonal region is used to correct the MC-based modelling of the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
background component. A control region enriched in
Z+\mathrm{jets}
events,
CR_{Z}
, is defined by requiring events to satisfy the common selection requirements and additionally contain two same-flavour leptons with opposite charge and with an invariant mass consistent with leptons arising from a
Z
boson decay,
81<m_{\ell\ell}<101

GeV
.

7
Observables

The primary observable used to separate signal from background is the invariant mass of the
t\bar{t}
system,
m_{t\bar{t}}
. Additionally, angular variables sensitive to the
t\bar{t}
spin correlations are used in both the 1- and 2-lepton channels to define validation regions for the SM
t\bar{t}
background and, in the case of the 2-lepton channel, to increase the sensitivity of the signal regions. The angular variables provide additional discrimination power between the signal process, in which the
t\bar{t}
system is produced via the
s
-channel diagram, and the background from SM
t\bar{t}
production, which mostly proceeds via the
t
-channel diagram.

7.1
Observables in the 1-lepton channel

The calculation of
m_{t\bar{t}}
requires the correct identification and reconstruction of all
t\bar{t}
decay products, including the four-momentum of the neutrino from the leptonic decay of one of the two top quarks. In both the resolved and the merged event topologies, the transverse momentum of the neutrino is taken to be the transverse missing momentum in the event,
\vec{p}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
. The four-momentum component longitudinal to the beam axis,
p_{z}^{\nu}
, is calculated from the kinematic constraint that the squared sum of the neutrino and charged lepton four-momenta must yield the squared mass of the
W
boson
[
16
]
. If the resulting equation has exactly one real solution, it is taken as the neutrino longitudinal momentum. If it has no real solutions, the
\vec{p}_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
vector is rescaled and rotated in the transverse plane by the minimal amount required to obtain exactly one real solution
[
19
]
. This procedure is based on the assumption that the lack of a real solution is caused by a mismeasurement of the transverse missing momentum. If two real solutions are found, in the resolved topology selection, the choice is made with a
\chi^{2}
algorithm, while in the merged-topology selection the solution with the smallest absolute value of
p_{z}^{\nu}
is chosen
[
19
]
.

7.1.1
Resolved topology

A
\chi^{2}
minimisation approach is used to select the four jets from the
t\bar{t}
decay from all selected small-
R
jets and assign them to the leptonically- and hadronically-decaying top quarks, identically to what is done in Ref.
[
24
]
. All possible jet permutations are tested, and the one with the lowest
\chi^{2}
is used; if there are two solutions for the neutrino longitudinal momentum, the one that yields the lowest
\chi^{2}
is kept. Any
b
-tagged jet can only be assigned to either of the
b
-quarks produced by the decay of a top quark, reducing the number of permutations to test. The experimental resolution of
m_{t\bar{t}}
in the resolved signal region is around 12% for
m_{t\bar{t}}^{\mathrm{afterFSR}}=400

GeV
and 10% for
m_{t\bar{t}}^{\mathrm{afterFSR}}=1000

GeV
.

7.1.2
Merged topology

For the merged topology, the hadronically-decaying top quark can be straightforwardly identified with the top-tagged large-
R
jet that passes the requirements in Section
5
. The leptonically-decaying top quark is reconstructed from the selected jet identified with the
b
-jet from the leptonic top-quark decay, the selected lepton, and the reconstructed neutrino (Section
6.1
). The
m_{t\bar{t}}
resolution for events in the merged signal region is around 10% for
m_{t\bar{t}}>600

GeV
.

In Figure
4
, the distributions of the reconstructed
t\bar{t}
invariant mass are shown for various representative resonance masses in the three benchmark models described in Section
2
. At low resonance masses, the
m_{t\bar{t}}
distributions show a clear peak at the resonance mass. At larger resonance masses, a tail towards masses lower than the generated mass is visible, which arises from off-shell production of the signal and the
growth of low-
x
partons in the proton PDF (where
x
stands for the Bjorken-
x
of the proton)
[
16
]
.

((a))

((b))

((c))

Figure 4:
Signal distributions in
m_{t\bar{t}}
after the signal region selection of the 1-lepton channel for the

Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
,

G_{\textrm{KK}}
, and

g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals for various representative values of the resonance mass. Events from the Resolved
1b
, Resolved
2b
, and Merged signal regions are included.

7.2
Observables in the 2-lepton channel

The invariant mass of the
t\bar{t}
system cannot be unambiguously reconstructed in the 2-lepton channel due to the presence of the two neutrinos from the two leptonically-decaying top quarks. Therefore, instead of the
t\bar{t}
invariant mass, the invariant mass of the two selected
b
-jets and the two charged leptons,
m_{\ell\ell bb}
, is used as the discriminating variable.

Figure
5
shows the distributions of the reconstructed
m_{\ell\ell bb}
discriminant for the same representative signal masses as shown in the 1-lepton channel. The same trend of low-mass tails for the high-mass signals that were seen in the
m_{t\bar{t}}
distributions are also visible in the
m_{\ell\ell bb}
distributions.

((a))

((b))

((c))
