Signal distributions in
m_{\ell\ell bb}
after the signal region selection of the 2-lepton channel for the

Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
,

G_{\textrm{KK}}
, and

g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals for various representative values of the resonance mass. Events from the five signal regions are combined.

8
Data-driven estimation of background contributions

8.1
W+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 1-lepton channel

Scale factors derived from data are applied before the final analysis to correct the normalisation of the
W+\mathrm{jets}
background in the
Sherpa
MC simulation samples, to account for possible mismodelling of the cross-section times acceptance. The scale factors are determined by comparing the measured
W
boson charge asymmetry in data with that predicted by the simulation. The total number of
W+\mathrm{jets}
events in data in a given signal region,
N_{W^{+}}+N_{W^{-}}
, is given by

N_{W^{+}}+N_{W^{-}}=\frac{r_{\mathrm{MC}}+1}{r_{\mathrm{MC}}-1}(D_{\mathrm{corr+}}-D_{\mathrm{corr-}})\,,

(1)

where
r_{\mathrm{MC}}
denotes the ratio of the number of
W+\mathrm{jets}
events with a positively-charged lepton to that with a negatively-charged lepton obtained from the
Sherpa
MC simulation.
D_{\mathrm{corr+(-)}}
is the number of observed data events with a positively-charged (negatively-charged) lepton. This method relies on the fact that the charge asymmetry obtained in MC simulation is in excellent agreement with the value measured in data
[
123
]
. Contributions to
D_{\mathrm{corr+(-)}}
from charge-asymmetric processes such as single-top,
WZ
, and
t\bar{t}+W
production are estimated from MC simulation and are subtracted from the data samples. Contributions from charge-symmetric processes such as
t\bar{t}
production and the signal process itself cancel out in the difference on the right-hand side of Eq. (
1
). The final scale factor,
C_{A}=1.125\pm 0.031
, is then calculated as the ratio of
N_{W^{+}}+N_{W^{-}}
evaluated from data to that predicted from the
Sherpa
MC simulation. The scale factor is evaluated for the combined
e+\mathrm{jets}
and
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
events channels in the merged and resolved regions combined; it was verified that the scale factors evaluated from the
e+\mathrm{jets}
and
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
channels separately are consistent.

The scale factors obtained in the method described above are applied to the single, combined template for the
W+\mathrm{jets}
background. A 50% uncertainty is assigned to the normalisation after the
C_{A}
correction to account for additional theory uncertainties related to the
W+\mathrm{jets}
sample, discussed further in Section
10.1.3
. Furthermore, the fraction of
W+\mathrm{HF}
in the
W+\mathrm{jets}
nominal template is varied by
\pm 50\%
, with this variation applied as an additional systematic uncertainty to cover any residual mismodelling arising from the relative flavour contributions
[
19
]
.

8.2
Fakes background in the 1-lepton channel

The fakes background in the 1-lepton channel consists of events with a non-prompt lepton or a jet mis-reconstructed as a lepton that satisfies the lepton identification and isolation criteria applied in this search (referred to as
tight
lepton requirements in the following). In the
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
channel, muons arising from semileptonic decays of hadrons constitute the main source of this background. In the
e+\mathrm{jets}
channel, additional fakes arise from events containing jets with a large electromagnetic component, for example from
\pi^{0}\rightarrow\gamma\gamma
decays, or photons mis-identified as isolated electrons. The normalisation,
t\bar{t}
invariant mass shape, statistical and systematic uncertainties associated with the fakes background are estimated from data using the
matrix method
. The matrix method used in this search is based on the one used in previous ATLAS
t\bar{t}
resonance searches and measurements
[
19
,
16
]
and is the same as that employed in Ref.
[
24
]
.

The number of events with leptons satisfying loose lepton identification and isolation criteria
[
118
]
,
N_{L}
, is defined as

N_{\mathrm{L}}=N_{\mathrm{prompt}}+N_{\mathrm{fakes}}\,,

(2)

where
N_{\mathrm{prompt}}
and
N_{\mathrm{fakes}}
denote the number of events with prompt leptons and events with leptons from other sources, respectively. The number of events with leptons satisfying the tight lepton identification criteria applied in the signal regions,
N_{T}
, can be written as

N_{\mathrm{T}}=\epsilon\times N_{\mathrm{prompt}}+f\times N_{\mathrm{fakes}}\,,

(3)

where the
real rate
,
\epsilon
, is an estimator for the probability of a prompt lepton passing the loose lepton identification criteria to also pass the tight critieria, and the
fake rate
,
f
, is an estimator for the probability that a lepton from other sources passing the loose criteria also passes the tight criteria. The fake rate is estimated from a control region, defined by applying the same selection criteria as the resolved signal region, but with the missing transverse momentum and transverse mass requirements inverted. The veto on events passing the merged topology selection criteria is also not imposed to define this control region, to increase the available data. Contributions from processes leading to prompt leptons are subtracted from the data in this fakes-enriched control region using their predictions from MC simulation. The real rate is determined using SM
t\bar{t}
MC samples. Solving Eqs. (
2
) and (
3
) simultaneously for
N_{\mathrm{prompt}}
and
N_{\mathrm{fakes}}
yields the fakes contribution in the given signal region. The fakes estimate is performed separately for the
e+\mathrm{jets}
and
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
events.

A smoothing procedure is applied to the fakes background templates in both the resolved and the merged signal regions to mitigate large statistical fluctuations due to the limited data. In the resolved region, a parabolic smoothing algorithm is applied, whereby the input distribution histogram is rebinned to achieve a smooth behaviour. The limited data has a larger effect in the high-
m_{t\bar{t}}
tails, and the parabolic smoothing used in the resolved regions was observed to be too agressive for the merged topology, removing all entries in the tail of the distribution. Therefore, a custom smoothing procedure, applied separately for the
e+\mathrm{jets}
and
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
channels, and separately in the signal and validation regions, was designed and implemented for the fakes background in the 1-lepton merged regions. The algorithm uses an analytic function fit to the unbinned templates to smooth the binned template, with care taken to ensure there is continuity in the function when considering
m_{t\bar{t}}
-dependent
\Delta\eta\left(\ell,t^{\mathrm{had}}\right)
requirement. The systematic uncertainty assigned to this smoothing procedure is described in Section
10.1.3
.

The fakes modelling before applying the smoothing is validated in separate dedicated validation regions, where only one of the
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
or
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
+
m_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{W}}
requirements is inverted. These validation regions contain a background composition that is more similar to that in the signal region, while still having an enhanced fakes contribution. Based on the modelling performance observed in these validation regions, a conservative 50% normalisation uncertainty that is decorrelated across signal regions is assigned to the fakes background, in addition to the uncertainties related to the estimate and smoothing procedures.

8.3
Z+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 2-lepton channel

Data-driven corrections are applied to the
Sherpa
MC simulation samples for the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 2-lepton channel before the final fit to correct the overall normalisation of this background component and the modelling of relevant kinematic variables. The corrections are derived in the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
enriched
CR_{Z}
, where disagreement in the modelling of the background prediction obtained with the
Sherpa
MC simulated samples is observed compared with data for two kinematic variables in particular: the transverse momentum of the di-lepton system,
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\ell\ell}
, and the invariant mass
m_{\ell\ell bb}
. In both cases, the mis-modelling exhibits a linear trend, with good agreement observed for small values of these variables and a linear increase in the difference between data and MC prediction towards higher values. As a result, a reweighting procedure in
m_{\ell\ell bb}
is used to correct the
Sherpa
MC samples for the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
background to the data in
CR_{Z}
. Each simulated event is assigned a multiplicative correction weight based on its value of
m_{\ell\ell bb}
:

w=a\cdot(1-b\cdot m_{\ell\ell bb})\,.

(4)

The correction factors
a
and
b
are derived from a maximum-likelihood fit to the
m_{\ell\ell bb}
distributions, taking into account only the statistical uncertainty in the MC predictions. The following values are obtained for the correction factors:

a=1.190\pm 0.003\,,\quad b=(-1.7\pm 0.1)\cdot 10^{-4}\;\mathrm{GeV}^{-1}\,.

Good agreement between the data and the SM expectation is observed for all relevant kinematic variables after applying the reweighting correction. To cover the residual non-closure seen in the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
control region after requiring 2
b
-jets, two uncertainties are introduced. The first is a flat 30% normalisation uncertainty, and the second is estimated by varying the slope of Eq. (
4
) by 30% of its best-fit value and obtaining the reweighted
m_{\ell\ell bb}
distribution in this case. The difference between the nominal and the varied reweighted
m_{\ell\ell bb}
distributions is taken as the systematic uncertainty.

9
Statistical analysis

The compatibility of the data with the SM prediction is quantified by a binned profile-likelihood fit
[
124
,
125
]
of the expected
m_{t\bar{t}}
(1-lepton channel) and
m_{\ell\ell bb}
(2-lepton channel) distributions to the observed distributions. The fit is performed simultaneously in the three signal regions of the 1-lepton channel and the five signal regions of the 2-lepton channel. The shape of the binned
m_{t\bar{t}}
or
m_{\ell\ell bb}
distributions is parameterised in terms of the signal strength,
\mu
, interpreted as the ratio of the observed and predicted number of signal events. The SM
t\bar{t}
normalisation is a free parameter in the fit, while the other simulated background processes are normalised to their theory predictions, with uncertainties as described in Section
10
.

Systematic uncertainties, described in Section
10
, are taken into account as nuisance parameters (NPs) constrained by Gaussian distributions. The Beeston-Barlow “lite” technique
[
126
]
is used to incorporate uncertainties due to the limited data available for the MC samples used in the analysis, referred to as
MC statistics
, where one additional NP with a Poissonian constraint is added per bin of the discriminant distribution to the likelihood function. The statistical uncertainty originating from the fakes background estimate is also accounted for in these same bin-by-bin NPs, see Section
10.1.3
below. The combination of the MC statistical uncertainty and the fakes background uncertainty is referred to as
background statistical uncertainties
in the remainder of this paper.

10
Systematic uncertainties

The modelling of signal and background events is affected by various experimental uncertainties related to the reconstruction, identification, and calibration of object and event properties. In addition, uncertainties related to the theory modelling of the simulated backgrounds, most importantly SM
t\bar{t}
production, are taken into account. The largest sources of systematic uncertainties in the analysis vary depending on the mass and width of the signal hypothesis being tested, but overall are related to the modelling of the SM
t\bar{t}
and the 1-lepton fakes background processes, followed by uncertainties in the jet energy scale and resolution. Table
6
in Section
11
summarises the main sources of uncertainty for three signal hypotheses and their impact on the observed signal strengths.

10.1
Modelling uncertainties

10.1.1
Uncertainties in the modelling of the SM
t\bar{t}
background

Modelling uncertainties are considered relating to the background from SM
t\bar{t}
production, which is estimated by using MC simulation at NLO+PS precision, and corrected to NNLO-QCD+NLO-EW calculations (see Section
4
). The background prediction for this process is hence subject to uncertainties in the higher-order calculations, uncertainties related to the reweighting procedure, and uncertainties due to modelling choices made in the NLO+PS simulation. As mentioned earlier, the impact of including a potential quasi-bound state consistent with that observed by CMS was found to be negligible.

The uncertainties in the higher-order calculations are assessed by variations of the renormalisation and factorisation scales, the PDF set, and the EW contribution, which is not present in the NLO+PS prediction. The scale uncertainties are estimated separately for the (anti-)top
p_{\textrm{T}}
and
m_{t\bar{t}}
distributions by varying the renormalisation and factorisation scales independently up and down by a factor of two, yielding four independent scale uncertainty components. The uncertainty in the PDF set is taken as the envelope of the intra-PDF uncertainties of the LUXQED PDF set
[
127
]
, which is used to obtain the NNLO-QCD+NLO-EW predictions. The uncertainty in the EW contribution is estimated by comparing the nominal spectra with those obtained with the NNPDF3.0QED PDF set
[
128
,
63
]
, which has been found to yield a significantly different prediction for the EW contributions due to a different treatment of the photon PDF compared to the nominal LUXQED PDF set
[
66
]
.

Two uncertainties are assigned to the iterative reweighting method. Possible biases due to the order in which the reweighting is applied on the
m_{t\bar{t}}
and (anti-)top quark
p_{\textrm{T}}
distributions is estimated by comparing the nominal reweighted sample with an alternative sample in which the reweighting is applied in a different order. The second uncertainty is derived to account for differences between the definitions of the parton-level top-quark: in the higher-order calculations, it includes at most two real emissions, whereas for the NLO MC generator, any number of real emissions can be included. To account for this, an alternative MC sample is created with the same generators and settings as the nominal NLO MC sample, but without decaying the top quarks and forbidding PS emissions after the first one. The nominal NLO sample is then reweighted to match the parton-level distributions of this modified sample, with the exact same procedure as the reweighting to the higher-order predictions. The difference between this reweighted sample and the un-reweighted sample is taken as the uncertainty in the observables at detector level.

Uncertainties due to the modelling choices made for the NLO+PS MC simulation are estimated by comparing the nominal SM
t\bar{t}
prediction to alternative predictions, obtained by either varying parameters within the nominal generators or with alternative MC generators. The alternative MC samples are reweighted to the respective higher-order predictions if the uncertainties they represent do not affect the parton-level variables used for the reweighting, namely
m_{t\bar{t}}
and the (anti-)top quark
p_{\textrm{T}}
. The following uncertainties in this group are:

•

the uncertainty related to the ME-PS matching, estimated by comparing the predictions obtained with the nominal sample with those obtained from an alternative sample generated with the same generator settings as the nominal sample but setting the
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{hard}}
parameter
4
4

4

The
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{hard}}
parameter regulates the definition of the vetoed region of the showering to avoid holes or overlaps in the phase space filled by
Powheg
and
Pythia
.
in
Pythia
to 1 instead of 0
[
129
]
;

•

the uncertainty related to the choice of the
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter, estimated by comparing the predictions of the nominal sample with those obtained with an alternative sample with the
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter increased by a factor of 1.5
m_{\mathrm{top}}
compared with its nominal value;

•

variations in the ISR ME calculation, which are estimated by varying the factorisation and renormalisation scales independently up and down by a factor of two; and,

•

variations in the FSR, assessed by varying the renormalisation scale for final-state PS emissions up and down by a factor of two.

For the cases of the ME-PS matching and the
h_{\text{damp}}
uncertainties, the resulting one-sided uncertainties are symmetrised to obtain effective
up
and
down
variations.

The uncertainty associated with variations of the renormalisation scale in the ISR PS is obtained via the corresponding systematic variation in the A14 tune. This variation does not have an explicit higher-order prediction so the nominal reweighting is applied instead, since the uncertainty also does not affect the parton-level variables used for the reweighting.

Finally, several modelling uncertainties are not reweighted at all and compared with the unreweighted nominal sample. These include:

•

the uncertainty related to the choice of the PS and hadronisation model, which is estimated by comparing the nominal predictions with those obtained from a sample generated at NLO in QCD with
Powheg Box
v2 with the same PDF set and
h_{\text{damp}}
value as in the nominal sample but interfaced with
Herwig
7.13
[
130
,
131
,
132
]
, using the
Herwig
7.1 default set of tuned parameters
[
131
,
132
]
;

•

the uncertainty related to the
t\bar{t}
lineshape, estimated by comparing the predictions obtained with the nominal sample to those obtained with an alternative sample generated with the same setup as the nominal sample but using
MadSpin
to decay the top quarks; and,

•

the uncertainty related to the choice of
m_{\mathrm{top}}
, estimated by comparing the nominal sample with
m_{\mathrm{top}}=172.5

GeV
to two alternative samples with
m_{\mathrm{top}}
values of 171.0
GeV
and 174.0
GeV
, respectively, and multiplying the difference by 0.2. This yields an uncertainty of
\pm 0.3

GeV
, corresponding ot the current world-average uncertainty in the top-quark mass
[
133
]
.

Again, for both the PS uncertainty and the lineshape uncertainty, the resulting one-sided uncertainties are symmetrised to obtain effective
up
and
down
variations.

10.1.2
Uncertainties in the modelling of the signal

Theory uncertainties relating to the scale and PDF+
\alpha_{s}
variations are taken into account for the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal processes. The impact of uncertainties related to the modelling of the signals on the acceptance times efficiency was assessed and found to be negligible compared with the uncertainties from instrumental sources.

10.1.3
Uncertainties in the modelling of the non-
t\bar{t}
backgrounds

The largest background contributions from processes other than SM
t\bar{t}
production arise from single-top quark production and, in the 1-lepton channel, from both
W+\mathrm{jets}
and fakes.

The main uncertainty in the modelling of single-top quark production is determined from a comparison of the diagram removal scheme and the alternative diagram subtraction scheme
[
75
,
64
]
for the treatment of interference effects and overlap between SM
t\bar{t}
and
Wt
production. It is estimated by comparing the nominal
Wt
samples, generated with the diagram removal scheme, to a set of alternative samples obtained with the diagram subtraction scheme, using the same generator settings as in the nominal single-top samples. As in the case of the SM
t\bar{t}
background, an uncertainty related to the choice of the PS and hadronisation model is estimated for the
Wt
background component by comparing the nominal prediction to that obtained with the same ME generator settings but interfaced with
Herwig
7.16, using the
Herwig
7.1 default set of tuned parameters
[
131
,
132
]
and the
MMHT2014lo
PDF set
[
134
]
. The uncertainty related to the ME-PS matching is estimated by comparing the predictions obtained with the nominal sample to those obtained from an alternative sample generated with the same generator settings as the nominal sample but setting the
p_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{hard}}
parameter in
Pythia
to 1 instead of 0. Modelling uncertainties related to the choice of the renormalisation and factorisation scales, the levels of ISR and FSR, and the choice of PDF set have also been evaluated but are found to be negligible. The theory uncertainties in the cross-sections for
Wt
-,
t
-, and
s
-channel production are applied as pure normalisation uncertainties in the respective components. They are estimated by summing in quadrature the scale and the PDF+
\alpha_{s}
uncertainties in the nominal cross-sections, and amount to
\pm 5.4\%
for
Wt
production
[
77
]
,
{}^{+4.3\%}_{-3.7\%}
for
t
-channel production
[
84
]
, and
{}^{+4.4\%}_{-4.1\%}
for
s
-channel production.

For the
W+\mathrm{jets}
production in the 1-lepton channel, a
\pm 50\%
normalisation uncertainty is assigned, and uncertainties related to the choice of scales and PDF set are taken into account as shape-only uncertainties for the MC modelling. To account for any residual mis-modelling of the flavour fractions in the
W+\mathrm{jets}
background after applying the correction (see Section
8.1
), the heavy-flavour fraction of the template is varied up and down by 50%, consistent with the corrections to the
W+\mathrm{jets}
template in Ref.
[
19
]
. In the 2-lepton channel, no separate uncertainty is assigned to the background from
W+\mathrm{jets}
production as it is included in the fakes background component, which has a 30% uncertainty applied to cover the disagreement observed between data and the MC simulation in a fake-enriched control region
5
5

5

The fake-enriched control region is defined by requiring two signal leptons with the same-sign electric charge and either
e\mu
or
\mu\mu
events, and that
m_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{W}}<100

GeV
for at least one of the
W
bosons in the event.
.

The systematic uncertainty applied to the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 2-lepton channel is related to the data-driven reweighting described in Section
8.3
. It consists of two components, one affecting the shape and the other affecting the normalisation of this background component. In the 1-lepton channel, where
Z+\mathrm{jets}
production constitutes an even smaller background component, a 30% normalisation uncertainty is applied, which covers both the
\pm 5
% uncertainty in the
Z+\mathrm{jets}
production cross-section and acceptance uncertainties related to possible mismodellings of higher jet multiplicities.

The uncertainty assigned to the data-driven estimate of the fakes background in the 1-lepton channel consists of three components:

•

a 50% normalisation uncertainty that is applied separately to the
e+\mathrm{jets}
and
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
contributions of the fakes estimate;

•

an uncertainty related to the statistical uncertainty in the original Matrix Method estimate, before smoothing; and,

•

an additional systematic uncertainty regulating the shape of the tail of the distribution in the merged regions, obtained from the smoothing procedure.

The dominant uncertainty in the fakes estimate is due to the limited data available for the data-driven estimate.

In the case of the remaining small backgrounds, only normalisation uncertainties are applied. For the backgrounds originating from
t\bar{t}+Z
,
t\bar{t}+W
, and
t\bar{t}+H
production, the uncertainties in the respective higher-order cross-sections are taken into account. They amount to
{}^{+10\%}_{-12\%}
,
{}^{+13\%}_{-12\%}
, and
{}^{+6.8\%}_{-9.8\%}
, respectively. A 50% normalisation uncertainty is applied to the diboson background to take into account any possible mismodelling in the production of additional jets
[
135
]
and heavy-flavour jets
[
136
]
.

10.2
Experimental uncertainties

The dominant experimental uncertainties affecting the
m_{t\bar{t}}
and
m_{\ell\ell bb}
spectra are the uncertainties in the jet energy scale (JES) and resolution (JER) of the small-
R
jets, for which there are 30 and 13 uncorrelated NPs included in the fit, respectively. Jet mass scale (JMS) uncertainties are also included for small-
R
jets but do not have a large impact. The small-
R
jet uncertainties are evaluated from a combination of simulations, test-beam data, and in situ measurements
[
104
]
. Additional contributions from jet flavour composition, punch-through, single-particle response, calorimeter response to different jet flavours and pile-up are also taken into account. Additionally, an uncertainty is assigned to the efficiency of the JVT requirement on jets
[
105
]
. For large-
R
jets, uncertainties in the JES, JER and JMS are derived in similar ways to small-
R
jets, using a combination of simulation, test-beam data, and in situ measurements
[
112
]
. Uncertainties related to jet mass resolution (JMR) are also included for large-
R
jets
[
112
]

Further experimental uncertainties in the correction factors for the
b
-tagging efficiencies
[
114
]
, as well as the rates of mis-tagging
c
-
[
115
]
and light-flavour jets
[
116
]
, derived from looking at dedicated flavour-enriched samples in data, are applied to the simulated event samples. An additional
b
-tagging efficiency uncertainty is applied for high-momentum jets (
p_{T}>400

GeV
) to account for uncertainties in the modelling of the track reconstruction in high-
p_{\textrm{T}}
environments
[
137
]
. Uncertainties are applied to large-
R
jets that satisfy the top-tagging requirements described in Section
5
. They are derived from a combination of measurements and account for background efficiency, signal efficiency, high-
p_{\textrm{T}}
extrapolation and inefficiency uncertainties. Correlations between with the JES and
b
-tagging uncertainties are taken into account
[
138
]
.

Smaller experimental uncertainties are related to the efficiencies of the lepton identification, isolation, and reconstruction, as well as the lepton energy scale and resolution
[
118
,
117
]
and to the scale and resolution of the track soft-term in the
E_{\mathrm{T}}^{\mathrm{miss}}
calculation
[
120
]
. Variations in the reweighting applied to simulated samples to match the expected mean number of interactions observed in each bunch crossing in data are included. They cover the uncertainty in the ratio between the predicted and measured inelastic cross-section. A constant 0.83% normalisation uncertainty is applied to all signal and background samples, except for fakes and
W+\mathrm{jets}
that are estimated from data, accounting for the uncertainty in the integrated luminosity.

10.3
Uncertainty correlation scheme

All experimental uncertainties are treated as fully correlated across samples and the signal regions of the 1- and 2-lepton channels in the final profile-likelihood fit (Section
11
). The modelling uncertainties are treated as uncorrelated between all background samples, but in general correlated across signal regions. However, the uncertainties in the SM
t\bar{t}
background related to the modelling of the PS and hadronisation, the PS-ME matching, the lineshape, the FSR and the choice of the
h_{\text{damp}}
parameter are treated as uncorrelated between all 3+5 signal regions of the 1- and 2-lepton channels, and separated into their shape and acceptance components. These uncertainties are obtained by comparing the nominal sample to an alternative SM
t\bar{t}
sample in each case (
two-point systematic uncertainties
), an approach that typically yields large pre-fit uncertainties that are often highly constrained in a profile likelihood fit. Keeping these uncertainties uncorrelated between all signal regions is chosen to prevent constraints propagating across regions, thus minimising the overall constraints on these NPs. Additionally, the uncertainties in the SM
t\bar{t}
background related to the choice of renormalisation and factorisation scales cannot be assumed to be fully correlated across different kinematic regimes, and are therefore treated as uncorrelated between the resolved and merged signal regions of the 1-lepton channel and between the 1- and 2-lepton channels, although they are treated as correlated across the different
b
-tagging bins of the 1-lepton channel and the different angular bins of the 2-lepton channel.

The uncertainty in the nomalisation factor assigned to the fakes background in the 1-lepton channel is uncorrelated across all three signal regions, and between the
e+\mathrm{jets}
and
\mu+\mathrm{jets}
channels. The normalisation factor and
W+\textrm{HF}
shape factor assigned to the
W+\mathrm{jets}
background in the 1-lepton channel are also treated as uncorrelated across the three signal regions. All other modelling NPs are treated as correlated across all signal regions.

11
Results

The data are confirmed to be consistent with the background-only hypothesis through a fit of the observed data to the SM prediction with
\mu=0
in the eight signal regions, where only the NPs are allowed to vary. The
m_{t\bar{t}}
distributions in the three signal regions of the 1-lepton channel and the
m_{\ell\ell bb}
distributions in the five signal regions of the 2-lepton channel after the fit to the full 140
fb
-1
data sample are shown in Figures
6
and
7
, respectively. Good compatibility of the observed
m_{t\bar{t}}
and
m_{\ell\ell bb}
spectra with the post-fit expected spectra is seen in all signal regions, with a goodness-of-fit probability of 95% obtained from a saturated model test
[
139
]
.

11.1
Uncertainty impact

The relative importance of the different categories of systematic uncertainties is quantified via their post-fit impact on the observed signal strength for three representative signal hypotheses. The results are shown for one narrow, low-mass
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal and one wide, low-mass
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signal, both of which are fully contained in the resolved signal regions, and one narrow, high-mass
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal, which is contained mostly in the merged signal region. The
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signals show the same trends as the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
, due to the similar widths and similar reconstructed
m_{t\bar{t}}
shapes; therefore, the discussion below on the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
uncertainties also apply to the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signals.

The impact of each NP on the signal strength is evaluated from the covariance matrix of the fit, and the impact of a group of NPs is obtained by summing in quadrature the impacts of all NPs in the respective category. Similarly, the total systematic uncertainty is obtained by summing in quadrature the impacts of all relevant NP groups. The statistical uncertainty is calculated by subtracting in quadrature the total systematic uncertainty from the total uncertainty in the signal strength. Table
6
summarises the impacts, where the fractional contribution to the total uncertainty in the observed signal strength of each category is given. The observed signal strength and the corresponding absolute uncertainty are shown in the top row.

Of the systematic uncertainties, the dominant impact on the post-fit result depends on the signal hypothesis being tested; however, the
t\bar{t}
background modelling uncertainties usually have relatively large impacts for all mass points. The largest experimental systematic uncertainties are those related to the jet energy scale and jet energy resolution of the small-
R
jets for the low-mass signals and to top-tagging for the high-mass signals, followed by flavour-tagging related systematic uncertainties. Besides the systematic uncertainties, the NPs related to the statistical uncertainty from the fakes background, included in “Modelling: statistical uncertainty” in Table
6
, are also important, and for some mass points, these are the among the parameters with the highest impact. For the highest mass regions, this is directly due to limited data. On the other hand, for the low-mass, narrow signals, the NPs related to the statistical uncertainty in the fakes are highly-correlated with the parameter of interest. This is because these NPs are completely uncorrelated across bins, and the width of the narrow signals is similar to the bin-width used. The total uncertainty in this analysis is still predominantly statistically dominated for the narrow resonances; however, for the wide resonances, the systematic uncertainties are comparable or even larger than the statistical uncertainties.

The details of how the impact of each NP on the signal strength is evaluated, as well as how the total systematic and statistical uncertainties are calculated, are given in the text. “Modelling: statistical uncertainty” refers to the contribution from the statistical uncertainties of the MC simulated samples and the data-driven background estimate for the 1-lepton fakes background. “Modelling: signal” refers to the theory uncertainties on the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal; these uncertainties are not considered for the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
and

11.2
Constraints on selected benchmark models

No significant excesses are observed across the entire
m_{t\bar{t}}
or
m_{\ell\ell bb}
spectra; therefore, upper limits at the 95% confidence level (CL) are derived on the production cross-section times branching ratio to
t\bar{t}
for the resonances predicted in the benchmark scenarios described in Section
2
. The upper limits are calculated using the
{\mathrm{CL}_{s}}
frequentist formalism
[
140
]
and the approximate limits for intermediate masses are interpolated between the tested mass hypotheses. For the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signal, the detector resolution is finer than the grid spacing, so the interpolation is only for display purposes.

The observed and expected exclusion limits are shown in Figure
10
for the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
,
g_{\textrm{KK}}
and
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signal hypotheses. The observed limits are in good agreement with the expected limits. Upper limits on the cross-section are extracted to be 3.6 pb, 1.8 pb, and 6.4 pb for a 500
GeV

Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
,
G_{\textrm{KK}}
, and
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signal, respectively. The results in this paper are the best limits on the cross-section times branching fraction to
t\bar{t}
for the
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal for
m_{t\bar{t}}<1.5

TeV
, and the best across the full
m_{t\bar{t}}
range considered for the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
and
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals to date.
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signals with a width of 3% (1.2%) and
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signals are excluded up to masses of 4.2
TeV
(3.65
TeV
) and 4.1
TeV
, respectively, while the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
is excluded up to 1.3
TeV
. The
g_{\textrm{KK}}
mass exclusions set the strongest limits on this benchmark model to date. The
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
mass exclusions significantly increase the previously excluded ATLAS limits in the 1-lepton channel on 36
fb
-1
of data; however, the ATLAS search in fully hadronic final states still provide the most stringent mass limits on these models
[
23
]
. The mass limit on the
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signal is the most stringent limit to date in the
t\bar{t}
decay channel, although complementary searches in diboson final states do provide stronger mass exclusions.

((b))

for the

,

12
Conclusion

The search presented in this paper targets heavy resonances decaying into top–antitop-quark pairs using final states with exactly one or two leptons. The analysis is performed using 140
fb
-1
of proton–proton collision data recorded by the ATLAS detector at the LHC at a centre-of-mass energy of
\sqrt{s}=13\penalty\ \text{TeV}
. The search covers resonance masses in the range of 0.4
TeV
to 5.0
TeV
and considers both narrow and broad resonances, relative to the detector resolution.

No significant deviation from the Standard Model background expectation is observed across the probed invariant mass spectrum. As a result, upper limits are set on the production cross-section times branching fraction for three benchmark scenarios. For
m_{t\bar{t}}<1.5

TeV
, the search sets the most stringent constraints to date on narrow
t\bar{t}
resonances, excluding, for instance, values of the production cross-section times branching fraction greater than 3.6 pb for a
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
with a mass of 500
GeV
.

The
Z^{\prime}_{\mathrm{TC2}}\,
signal with a relative width of 3% (1.2%) is excluded for masses below 4.2
TeV
(3.65
TeV
). These limits are slightly weaker than those obtained in the ATLAS analysis targeting fully-hadronic final states, which excluded masses up to 4.7
TeV
using the same data, yet the present results offer complementary sensitivity in alternative
t\bar{t}
decay channels.

Furthermore, the analysis yields the strongest exclusions to date for the
g_{\textrm{KK}}
signal with a relative width of 30%, excluding those with a mass below 4.1
TeV
. The constraints on
G_{\textrm{KK}}
signals are the strongest to date in the studied decay channels, ruling out masses below 1.3
TeV
and significantly extending the reach of previously published results in the
t\bar{t}
decay channel.

Overall, the results reported in this study enhance existing exclusion limits across a broad range of models predicting heavy resonances decaying into top quark pairs.

The ATLAS Collaboration

G. Aad
{}^{\textrm{\scriptsize 103}}
,
E. Aakvaag
{}^{\textrm{\scriptsize 17}}
,
B. Abbott
